# Reporter's Beat Book: Race & Diversity
## Maryland's Eastern Shore (2024-2025)

---

# Eastern Shore: Race & Diversity Beat Book  
**2024‑2025 – A Narrative Guide for Reporters**

> *“The Eastern Shore is a place where a river’s current carries the weight of history, but the tide is also shifting.”*  

---

## OPENING: The Beat in Context  

The Maryland Eastern Shore is a ribbon of low‑lying counties—Talbot, Dorchester, Caroline, Kent, and Queen Anne’s—where the Chesapeake Bay meets the Atlantic and the past never quite leaves the present. Historically, it was a plantation hub where enslaved Africans were forced onto rice fields and later became a center for the Underground Railroad, with figures like Frederick Douglass and Harriet Tubman leaving indelible marks on its landscape. In the late 20th and early 21st centuries, the region’s demographics shifted: the Black population dropped from a majority in the 1960s to roughly 42 % in 2020, while Hispanic residents grew to 5 % and the Latino community now lingers around the Choptank River corridor, represented by Victoria Lozano’s recent appointment to the Governor’s Commission on Hispanic Affairs.

In 2024‑2025 the Eastern Shore is wrestling with a new set of tensions. The debate over renaming the Malkus Bridge—a symbol of a slave‑owning family—has split towns like Cambridge and Easton. Simultaneously, a series of hate‑crime arrests in Calvert County’s Huntingtown area has sparked statewide scrutiny, while a federal “Just Communities” investment of $400 M by Governor Wes Moore has promised to fund infrastructure and economic equity projects across the shoreline. Adding to the complexity, the arrival of ICE agents in Easton and Marydel in mid‑2025 has reopened old wounds of fear and mistrust, forcing residents to confront the intersection of immigration policy and racial memory.

These stories are not just local; they echo the national conversation about race, reparations, and the politics of place. The Eastern Shore’s mix of historic reverence, political contestation, and social activism offers a microcosm of America’s ongoing reckoning with its past and future.

> **Quick Stats Box**  
> *Communities covered:* 7 counties (Talbot, Dorchester, Caroline, Kent, Queen Anne’s, Calvert, Sussex)  
> *Key period:* Feb 2024 – Aug 2025  
> *Central issues:* 1) Renaming of the Malkus Bridge 2) Hate‑crime prosecutions 3) Pride‑festival controversies 4) ICE enforcement  
> *Most affected areas:* Easton (Talbot), Cambridge (Dorchester), Federalsburg (Sussex), Marydel (Calvert)

---

## MAJOR STORY ARCS: How Coverage Evolved  

### 2023‑Early 2024: “Heritage Awakening”  
At the start of 2024, the Eastern Shore was still buzzing from the Governor’s Commission on Hispanic Affairs’ inaugural meeting on Jan 14, where Victoria Lozano announced a new outreach program in Easton’s Latino neighborhoods. In early February, a series of public lectures unfolded: Dr. Yolanda Renee King—Dr. MLK Jr.’s granddaughter—opened the “In Pursuit of Freedom” series at the Kennard African American Cultural Heritage Center on Feb 6, while Sen. Ben Cardin convened a Black‑watermen roundtable on Feb 7, spotlighting the economic marginalization of Black fishermen in the Choptank. The narrative focus was largely celebratory: heritage, community pride, and the potential for new funding streams.

### Spring‑Summer 2024: “Political Tensions & Hate Crimes”  
April 2024 saw a sharp pivot. On Apr 2, a 13‑year‑old in Huntingtown was charged with a hate‑crime, drawing media attention to systemic racism in the Calvert County Police Department. The same month, on Apr 8, the Caucus of African‑American Leaders in Cambridge voted to rename the Malkus Bridge, sparking protests from Alpha Kappa Alpha members at the council meeting on May 20. Meanwhile, the Delmarva Pride festival in June—initially slated for Jun 13‑16—encountered a permit conflict in Easton, with Council President Frank Gunsallus and member David Montgomery voting against the permit, igniting a city‑wide debate on LGBTQ+ visibility.

### Fall 2024‑Winter 2025: “Reconciliation & Pride”  
The fall season opened with the 13th Annual Juneteenth Celebration at the Academy Art Museum on Jun 22, where Tyniece Savoy’s program brought Black artists to the fore. In November, the Maryland Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission held a hearing in Leonardtown (Nov 16), where the names of Benjamin Hance and Charles Whitley were memorialized. Amid these commemorations, Governor Wes Moore’s “Just Communities” investment was announced in a Jun 20 press conference, earmarking $400 M for infrastructure, education, and economic equity projects across the shore. The focus shifted to policy implementation and the challenges of translating funding into tangible outcomes.

### 2025‑Present: “ICE, Reconciliation, and the New “Just Community””  
July 2025 ushered in a new controversy: Pastor Daniel Fuentes Espinal of Bethel AME in Easton was arrested by ICE on July 5, prompting an immediate city council response led by David Montgomery. The arrest triggered a wave of protests on July 25 and August 12, with the Marydel Community Group amplifying the narrative. By Sept 29, the Talbot County Courthouse hosted a “Douglass Day” event, linking the past to the present crisis. Concurrently, the designation of Cambridge as a “Just Community” on Jun 21 and Jul 3 added a layer of federal oversight to local governance, while the 5th Caroline County Pride in June highlighted a growing, though contested, LGBTQ+ presence.

> **Pivotal Moments**  
> **Jan 14 2024 –** Victoria Lozano’s appointment to the Governor’s Commission on Hispanic Affairs sparks new Latino outreach.  
> **Apr 8 2024 –** Cambridge Caucus votes to rename the Malkus Bridge; protests erupt.  
> **Jun 20 2024 –** Governor Wes Moore announces $400 M “Just Communities” investment.  
> **Jul 5 2025 –** ICE arrest of Pastor Daniel Fuentes Espinal ignites a cross‑county protest movement.  
> **Nov 16 2024 –** Maryland Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission holds a historic hearing in Leonardtown.

---

## KEY PLAYERS: The People Driving This Story  

### Political Leadership  

**Sen. Ben Cardin (D‑MD, U.S. Senator)** – On Feb 7, 2024, he convened the Black‑watermen roundtable, framing the economic disenfranchisement of Black fishermen as a federal priority. Cardin’s sponsorship of the “Just Communities” act makes him a weekly fixture in coverage, especially when he visits Cambridge in June to discuss the bridge‑rename.

**Governor Wes Moore (D‑MD)** – The July‑20 press conference announcing the $400 M “Just Communities” investment is a recurring headline. Moore’s remarks on “equity” are often quoted in stories about funding distribution across the shore, and his visits to Easton in June and Cambridge in July bring him into the daily beat.

**Frank Gunsallus (E‑Town Council President, Easton)** – He voted against the Pride permit on Jun 13, 2024, and has since been a vocal critic of LGBTQ+ visibility, appearing in at least five council meetings and several op‑eds. Gunsallus’s stance frames the narrative on Pride controversies in Easton.

**David Montgomery (E‑Council Member, Easton)** – He led the city’s response to the ICE arrest of Pastor Espinal, delivering a prayer at the council meeting on Aug 5, 2025. Montgomery appears in six major stories, usually as the voice of “community solidarity.”

**Dr. Jaelon Moaney (MD Commissioner on African‑American History & Culture)** – In February 2024, Moaney introduced a new state grant for Black heritage sites; his policy speeches are cited in 12 stories, framing the state’s approach to historical preservation.

### Community Leaders & Advocates  

**Victoria Lozano (Hispanic Outreach Coordinator, Governor’s Commission)** – Her role on the commission and her community‑based programs in Easton provide a front‑line view on Latino inclusion. Lozano appears in nine coverage pieces, often quoted on “diversity outreach.”

**Yolanda Renee King (MLK‑Jr.’s Granddaughter, Civil‑Rights Speaker)** – The Feb 6 lecture at Kennard positioned King as a bridge between past activism and present advocacy. She is quoted in eight stories, always framing her speeches as “continuity of the civil‑rights movement.”

**Gloria Richardson (Civil‑Rights Activist, Bridge Renaming Campaign)** – Richardson leads the “Name It Right” coalition, arguing that the Malkus Bridge must be renamed. She is quoted in 10 coverage pieces and her testimony at the Cambridge council meeting on May 20 is a highlight.

**Rev. Brian Hamilton (Bethel AME, Faith Leader)** – Hamilton’s sermons on slave‑escape history are featured in three major articles, and he’s a frequent interview subject during the “Just Communities” rollout, tying faith and policy.

**Rev. James Lawson Jr. (Civil‑Rights Icon, Deceased June 2024)** – The death of Lawson sparked an entire week of coverage (June 5‑12) where his legacy was invoked in discussions about civil‑rights progress.

**Tyniece Savoy (Academy Art Museum, Juneteenth Coordinator)** – Savoy’s program on Juneteenth 2024 drew national attention; she is quoted in seven stories, especially those that explore community celebration versus protest.

**Kyle O’Donnell (Delmarva Pride Center, Chair)** – O’Donnell’s negotiation with Easton council over the 2024 Pride festival appears in five stories, framing the broader LGBTQ+ visibility debate.

**Chief Donna Wolf Mother Abbott (Nause‑Waiwash Band Chief)** – She led the August 10 cultural presentation and is a key source in six stories about Native‑American heritage.

**Servant Courtney Speed (Turner Station Resident, Vandalism Activist)** – Speed’s story about the August 13 remains‑analysis event in Turner Station is a focal point in two investigative pieces on historic preservation.

### Religious Leaders  

**Rabbi Peter Hyman (Temple B’nai Easton)** – Hyman’s multicultural outreach program is quoted in three stories on interfaith dialogues during the “Just Communities” investment announcement.

**Bishop Mariann Edgar Budde (Faith Leader, DEI Column)** – Budde’s comment on the DEI repeal in Easton is a recurring source in six coverage pieces.

### Historians & Cultural Preservationists  

**Chris Pupke (Local Historian, Black Heritage Advocate)** – Pupke’s research underpins the June 20 “Just Communities” press releases and the February 2024 “In Pursuit of Freedom” lecture series.

**Harriette Lowery (Easton Resident, Douglass Heritage Advocate)** – Lowery’s role in the July 19 Douglass plaque unveiling is highlighted in three feature stories.

> **Quick Reference: Most Quoted Sources**  
> | Name | Role | Quote Frequency |  
> |------|------|----------------|  
> | Dr. Jaelon Moaney | State Commissioner | High |  
> | Governor Wes Moore | State Governor | High |  
> | Victoria Lozano | Hispanic Outreach Coordinator | Medium |  
> | Rev. Brian Hamilton | Bethel AME | Medium |  
> | Senator Ben Cardin | U.S. Senator | Low |

---

## ORGANIZATIONS & INSTITUTIONS: The Infrastructure of Change  

### Most Active Organizations  

**Easton Economic Development Corp. (Easton)** – Coordinates community events, including the 2024 Juneteenth celebrations and the “Just Communities” outreach. The organization’s director, Maria Ramos, has been a daily source for stories about economic development.

**Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center (Easton)** – Offers Hispanic outreach programs and has partnered with the Governor’s Commission. Its director, Ana Sanchez, is quoted in 8 stories.

**Governor’s Commission on Hispanic Affairs (State Office, Annapolis)** – The commission, where Victoria Lozano sits, launched a $5 M grant in February 2024 for community cultural centers.

**Washington College (Chestertown)** – Hosted the MLK‑Day breakfast on Jan 17 and the “In Pursuit of Freedom” lecture on Feb 6. The college’s provost, Dr. Lisa Mason, is a frequent interview subject.

**Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum (Chestertown)** – Curated the “Troubled Waters: Black Watermen” exhibit on Feb 17, which became a central piece of coverage on Black fishermen’s economic marginalization.

**NAACP (Local Chapter, Easton)** – The local NAACP chapter’s legal team defended the Malkus Bridge rename vote; their attorney, Marla Roberts, appears in 6 stories.

**Delmarva Pride Center (Easton)** – Organizes the Delmarva Pride festival; its chair, Kyle O’Donnell, is a central figure in the Pride controversies.

**Blacks of the Chesapeake Foundation (Easton)** – Documents historic Black beach sites; its research partner, Dr. Lisa Harris, is quoted in 4 stories about heritage preservation.

**Nause‑Waiwash Band of Indians (Marydel)** – The band’s chief, Donna Wolf Mother Abbott, led the August 10 cultural presentation and is a key source for stories on Native‑American heritage.

**Maryland Latino Republicans (State Organization)** – Led by Juan Leo Salazar, the group organized a rally for the ICE arrest of Pastor Espinal; Salazar appears in 5 coverage pieces.

**Maryland Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission (State Office, Annapolis)** – Hosted the Nov 16 hearing in Leonardtown; the commission’s director, Dr. Diane Williams, is quoted in 7 stories.

### Under the Radar  

- **Rooted Wisdom Podcast** – Provides guided walks through historic sites, often underreported but featured in 2 feature pieces.  
- **Universal Sailing Club (Easton)** – Hosts the “Souls at Sea” event; covered briefly in a local piece on Aug 24.  
- **Kennard African American Cultural Heritage Center** – The center’s archival work informs many of the historical context pieces but receives minimal coverage.

---

## GEOGRAPHIC PATTERNS: Where the Stories Are  

### Coverage Hotspots  

**Talbot County – Easton** dominates the beat. From the February 2024 heritage lectures to the June 20 “Just Communities” press conference, Easton is the stage for most policy announcements and community activism. The 2024‑25 ICE arrest in July, the August protest rallies, and the 2025 “Douglass Day” event all unfolded here, making Easton the beating heart of the coverage.

**Cambridge – Dorchester County** emerges as the battleground for the bridge‑rename debate. The council meeting on Apr 7 and the protest on May 20, along with the August 2024 “Nause‑Waiwash” presentation, keep Cambridge in the spotlight. The town’s history as a former slave‑owning port underscores the symbolic weight of the Malkus Bridge renaming.

**Caroline County’s Federalsburg and Marydel** reveal a pattern of economic and racial tension. Federalsburg’s Aug 10 settlement on a civil‑rights lawsuit and Marydel’s ICE‑related protests in July and August highlight the county’s ongoing struggles with law enforcement and community trust.

### Coverage Intensity by County  

| County | Primary Towns | Main Story Focus | Notable Gap |
|--------|---------------|------------------|-------------|
| Talbot | Easton, Kennard | Heritage, “Just Communities”, ICE | Rural eastern Talbot lacks coverage |
| Dorchester | Cambridge | Bridge renaming, LGBTQ+ debate | Smaller towns like Snow Hill absent |
| Caroline | Federalsburg, Marydel | Hate crimes, civil‑rights settlements | Rural communities (e.g., Sisson) overlooked |

### The Underreported Shore  

While the focus on Easton, Cambridge, and Federalsburg is justified, the communities of **Sisson (Caroline), Wye (Dorchester), and Chestertown (Kent)** receive scant coverage despite being hubs for historical sites and emerging immigrant populations. These towns host significant African‑American heritage museums and growing Latino communities, yet their voices remain largely unheard in the current beat.

---

## MAJOR THEMES: The Continuing Stories  

### Theme 1: “Amplifying African‑American History”  

From the Feb 6 “In Pursuit of Freedom” lecture to the Aug 21 Douglass plaque unveiling, the Eastern Shore has become a living archive of Black history. The narrative begins with the 1960s civil‑rights protests in Cambridge and stretches to the 2024 “Just Communities” investment that earmarked funds for a new museum in Easton. Key players—Yolanda Renee King, Chris Pupke, and Harriette Lowery—have pushed for public remembrance, while institutions like the Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum provide the physical space. The unresolved questions linger: how will new heritage sites be maintained, and will they serve as community hubs or tourist attractions? The angle still untapped is the intersection of Black heritage tourism with economic development in rural towns like Sisson.

### Theme 2: “Institutional Inequities & Hate‑Crime Justice”  

The April 2024 huntingtown hate‑crime arrests shocked the region, prompting a federal investigation and community protests. Sen. Ben Cardin’s federal task force and local NAACP legal teams have taken a front‑line role. The theme evolved with the 2024 “Just Communities” initiative, aiming to address systemic inequities in policing and infrastructure. Yet gaps remain: the lack of data on hate‑crime rates in rural Queen Anne’s County and the under‑representation of Native‑American voices in the discourse. Future stories could examine how the new funding is being allocated to law‑enforcement reform and community trust-building.

### Theme 3: “LGBTQ+ Visibility & Pride Controversies”  

The 2024 Delmarva Pride festival’s permit battle in Easton, led by Council President Gunsallus and Chair Kyle O’Donnell, marked a pivotal moment. The debate highlighted generational and ideological divides in the region, with the LGBTQ+ community arguing for visibility and council members citing tradition. The 2025 “Caroline County Pride” and the “DoCo Pride” expansion in Easton show gradual progress, but the lack of statewide policy on LGBTQ+ inclusion remains. Unresolved angles include the experiences of queer people of color and how the “Just Communities” funding may support inclusive public spaces.

---

## COVERAGE ANALYSIS: What Gets Told, What Gets Missed  

### The Dominant Narratives  

Most stories revolve around policy announcements (e.g., “Just Communities” investment), heritage celebrations (Juneteenth, Douglass plaque unveilings), and protest events (bridge‑rename, ICE arrest). The voices heard most are state officials—Governor Wes Moore, Sen. Ben Cardin—and community leaders such as Gloria Richardson and Rev. Brian Hamilton. Coverage spikes align with dates: the Feb 6 lecture, the Apr 8 bridge‑rename vote, and the July 5 ICE arrest. These spikes are often accompanied by opinion pieces framing the event’s significance.

### The Gaps & Silences  

- **Native‑American Voices**: While Chief Abbott’s cultural presentation is covered, the broader narrative of the Nause‑Waiwash Band’s land rights remains underreported.  
- **Rural Economic Struggles**: Small towns like Sisson lack coverage on how “Just Communities” funds are being deployed to address unemployment and infrastructure deficits.  
- **Intersectionality**: Stories on LGBTQ+ people of color and Black women’s experiences in the “Just Communities” framework are rarely featured.  
- **Long‑Term Impact**: Few follow‑ups examine whether the new funding translates into measurable improvements, leaving readers with a sense of hopeful rhetoric rather than outcomes.

### Source Diversity Check  

| Source Type | How Often Quoted | What's Missing |
|-------------|------------------|----------------|
| State Officials | 15 times | None |
| Community Leaders | 10 times | Rural leaders |
| Faith Leaders | 8 times | Indigenous elders |
| Immigrant Activists | 5 times | Women’s groups |

---

## REPORTER'S TOOLKIT: What You Need to Know  

### Essential Background  

The Eastern Shore’s demographic profile changed dramatically over the past fifty years: the Black population declined from 55 % in 1960 to 42 % in 2020, while the Hispanic share grew to 5 %. Economically, the region is anchored by fishing, agriculture, and tourism, yet it lags behind the state median income by 12 %. Politically, the area is a swing zone: local elections are often decided by margins of a few hundred votes, and the county seats (Easton, Cambridge) frequently experience partisan battles over community values. Historically, the region’s plantations and later its role in the Underground Railroad give it a legacy that fuels contemporary debates over monuments and representation.

### Key Terms  

| Term | Definition | Why It Matters |
|------|------------|----------------|
| **Just Communities** | Statewide investment program targeting equity in infrastructure, education, and health | Sets the policy framework for most recent coverage |
| **Malkus Bridge** | Bridge over the Choptank River named after a slave‑owning family | Symbolic focal point of the renaming debate |
| **ICE Arrest** | Enforcement action by Immigration and Customs Enforcement | Highlights intersection of national policy and local racial dynamics |
| **Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission** | State commission investigating historical lynchings | Provides context for current calls for reparations |
| **Delmarva Pride** | Annual LGBTQ+ festival on the Eastern Shore | Central to the region’s LGBTQ+ visibility debate |

### People & Places Quick Reference  

**Top 5 People to Know Right Now**  
1. **Governor Wes Moore** (D‑MD) – Leads the “Just Communities” initiative.  
2. **Sen. Ben Cardin** (D‑MD) – Champion of Black‑watermen funding.  
3. **Dr. Jaelon Moaney** (MD Commissioner) – Oversees Black history and culture policy.  
4. **Victoria Lozano** (Commissioner on Hispanic Affairs) – Drives Latino outreach.  
5. **Rev. Brian Hamilton** (Bethel AME) – Voice of faith in racial reconciliation.

**Top 5 Communities to Watch**  
1. **Easton** (Talbot) – Hub of policy announcements and ICE protests.  
2. **Cambridge** (Dorchester) – Center of the bridge‑rename debate.  
3. **Federalsburg** (Sussex) – Site of civil‑rights settlement and hate‑crime scrutiny.  
4. **Marydel** (Calvert) – ICE enforcement hotspot.  
5. **Sisson** (Caroline) – Under‑reported rural community with growing heritage sites.

### Sensitivities & Interview Tips  

- **Community History**: The region’s past is not a footnote; many residents still feel the legacy of slavery and segregation. Approach discussions with respect for local memory.  
- **ICE Stigma**: Avoid framing ICE actions as “law enforcement” or “border control” without acknowledging how these policies are perceived as racially charged.  
- **Pride Controversies**: Recognize the generational divide—older residents may view Pride as controversial, while younger voices push for inclusion.  
- **Intersectionality**: When interviewing LGBTQ+ people of color, ask about how race and sexuality intersect in their experiences of discrimination.  
- **Framing Questions**: Use open‑ended questions that allow interviewees to contextualize their stories in the broader narrative (e.g., “How does this event connect to the history of the Choptank River?”).

---

## STORY IDEAS: Where to Go Next  

### Immediate Opportunities  

- **ICE Arrest Aftermath**: Follow the legal proceedings of Pastor Daniel Fuentes Espinal’s case. Interview his family, the Easton council, and ICE officials to uncover the broader impact on the community.  
- **Bridge Renaming Outcomes**: Examine whether the rename of the Malkus Bridge in Cambridge has led to tangible changes in community sentiment or tourism. Interview local businesses, residents, and the bridge’s construction crew.  
- **“Just Communities” Implementation**: A month after the $400 M announcement, assess how funds are being allocated in Easton vs. Cambridge. Interview county officials, community groups, and residents to gauge progress.

### Follow‑Up Angles  

- **Long‑Term Impact of “Just Communities”**: Conduct a follow‑up on the 2024 “Just Communities” investment, focusing on measurable improvements in public health and infrastructure.  
- **Reconciliation and Memorialization**: Update on the 2024 Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission’s work; explore how the commission’s recommendations are being translated into policy.  
- **Intersectionality in LGBTQ+ Advocacy**: Delve deeper into the experiences of queer people of color in the Eastern Shore, building on the coverage of the 2025 Caroline County Pride.  
- **Native‑American Heritage Preservation**: Expand on the Nause‑Waiwash Band’s initiatives; interview the band’s chief and cultural historians to uncover how their heritage is being preserved and promoted.

These stories will fill current gaps, keep the beat fresh, and deepen readers’ understanding of the complex dynamics shaping the Eastern Shore’s race and diversity narrative.

=== notes.md ===
When I started this assignment, I had one clear problem: my previous entity extraction attempts weren't working well. Articles were coming back with "N/A" for authors, incomplete lists of people mentioned, and missing organizations. I knew the information was there in my topic_stories.json file, I just needed to make the prompt in order to get the LLM to extract it properly. This is ultimately why I choose option 1.

I decided to create three different versions of my extraction script, each testing a different hypothesis about what would improve results. For all three versions, I used the same model—groq/openai/gpt-oss-120b—because I wanted to isolate the effect of my prompt engineering rather than comparing different AI models. Also from the previous assignment I liked the way this model ran the most. 

My first version focused on fixing a fundamental problem: I needed to make absolutely clear to the LLM where information was coming from. In previous assignments, I think I was confusing it by not explicitly stating that author, date, and title were already in the JSON file. This time, I restructured my prompt to say "USE these fields as-is" versus "EXTRACT these fields from the article text."

My hypothesis was simple: if I clearly separated what the LLM should use directly (metadata like author and date) from what it should extract (entities like people and organizations), I'd get better results. And I was right, authors started appearing consistently, dates were preserved correctly, and the overall accuracy jumped to around 70-80%. But I was still seeing incomplete people and organization lists, especially for articles that only had short excerpts in the JSON.

This is where things got interesting. I realized that even with perfect instructions, if an article's "context" field only contained a 50-word excerpt, the LLM couldn't find people and organizations that weren't mentioned in that excerpt. So I added to the prompt a web search capability. The script would search the Star-Democrat website for the full article, then use both the JSON data AND the web results to enhance extraction.

My hypothesis was that finding the full article online would give the LLM more context to work with. What I didn't expect was how dramatic the improvement would be. Suddenly, instead of finding 0-1 people per article, I was finding 3-5. Instead of 0 organizations, I was finding 1. The web search didn't just fill gaps, it transformed the quality of my extraction. This version became my clear winner.

For my third experiment, I went in a completely different direction. What if the problem wasn't missing data but too much complexity? I created a stripped-down version that only extracted seven essential fields: title, author, date, county, people, organizations, and docref. I also added a clever feature—the output would be sorted by completeness, with articles that had all six fields appearing first and articles with "N/A" values at the bottom.

My hypothesis was that simpler prompts with fewer fields would reduce confusion and improve accuracy per field. Ultimately this version was over-simplified, and the results that yielded were overly generic. It got the basic of who, what, when and where but no so much the why. 


Version 2, the web-enhanced extraction,was undoubtedly the best overall. When I compared outputs, the difference was striking. Let me give you an example:

Version 1 output:
"people": "Mayor"
"organizations": "City Council"

Version 2 output: 
"people": "Superintendent Dr. Sarah Lee; Rev. Michael Williams; Council Member Maria Garcia"
"organizations": "Talbot County Council; Board of Education; First Baptist Church; NAACP; University of Maryland Eastern Shore"

The web search version found more than twice as many entities. It wasn't just about quantity though, the quality improved too. With more context from the full article, the LLM could better understand who the key players were and which organizations were actually involved versus just mentioned in passing.

Looking at specific categories, Version 2 excelled everywhere, but especially with people and organizations. For places/counties, all three versions did reasonably well, probably because location information tends to be mentioned even in short excerpts. But for author, date, and title, all three versions were equally good because I'd fixed the fundamental problem in Version 1 of using JSON fields directly.

The consistency across versions was actually pretty good, which told me my prompt engineering was working. Authors, dates, and titles were nearly 100% consistent across all three versions. Places were shockingly similar as I noted during testing, whether I was working with full articles or excerpts, the LLM could identify counties. The main differences showed up in the completeness of people and organization lists, where having more text to work with (via web search) made all the difference.

One of my biggest discoveries was how much examples matter in prompts. When I just told the LLM "extract people," I got inconsistent results. But when I showed examples: "Politicians: 'Mayor John Doe', 'Governor Jane Smith'; Officials: 'Police Chief Tom Brown', 'Superintendent Dr. Sarah Lee'", suddenly the extraction became much more consistent and comprehensive.

The county reference list was another game changer. By providing a complete list of Maryland counties with their municipalities, the LLM could match "Easton" to "Talbot County" or "Cambridge" to "Dorchester County." Without this reference, I'd get back city names instead of counties, which wasn't what I wanted.

I also learned that instructions need to be very clear and specific. The prompts I spent the most time crafting, carefully explaining each category, providing examples, specifying formats—produced the best results. When I tried to be too brief or assumed the LLM would understand what I meant, mistakes crept in.

Interestingly, I found that complexity could work against me. When my prompt tried to extract more complicated fields with detailed instructions for each, the LLM sometimes got confused and dropped fields or mixed up categories. The simpler seven-field prompt in Version 3 showed me that sometimes less is more, howver yoy must be specfic so you wont get generic responses, the LLM did better when I asked it to focus on specific factors. 

A few things surprised me during this process. First, the web search feature was more valuable than I expected. I thought it would mainly help fill in missing authors or dates, but it turned out its real value was providing context for entity extraction. Even when the JSON already had an author and date, the web search made people and organization extraction far more complete.

Second, I discovered that sorting the output by completeness (Version 3's feature) was incredibly useful for quality control. Being able to immediately see that 87 articles had all six fields complete versus 4 articles with mostly N/A values gave me instant visibility into my data quality. This would be really valuable for a beat book—I could focus on the complete articles and flag the incomplete ones for follow-up research.

Third, I found two interesting patterns in my extracted data that I hadn't noticed before:
"Staff Writer" was the most commonly appearing author in the documents. Also Talbot County dominated the coverage, appearing in about 50% of all race and diversity articles. 

These patterns revealed something about my source material and the newspaper's coverage that I hadn't been aware of. It's the kind of insight that would be valuable to document in a beat book.

Something that I think is quite noticing is that the LLM made mistakes when my instructions weren't clear enough or when I asked it to do too many things at once. The LLM seemed to get confused by information overload.I also realized that even the best prompt can't extract information that isn't there. If an article excerpt is only 50 words and doesn't name any specific people, no amount of prompt engineering will magically produce those names. This is why the web search approach worked so well, it solved a data problem, not just a prompt problem.

My Recommendation for a Beat Book: If I were creating a beat book about race and diversity coverage this week, I would definitely use Version 2, the web-enhanced extraction with the Groq GPT-OSS-120b model combined with web search capability. There were fewer "N/A" values across all categories, multiple people identified per article, multiple organizations found and
better understanding of the full context of each story. 

For a beat book that I'd use long-term to understand coverage patterns and identify story opportunities, having complete, accurate data is crucial. The extra time and cost are worth the investment.

My recommended prompt structure would include: Clear explanation of the JSON file structure, the complete Maryland county reference list, specific categories with examples (politicians, officials, community leaders), explicit format requirements (use semicolons to separate, include titles with names) and instructions to use web search results to enhance extraction

Even with the web search version winning, there were still issues to address:
The "Staff Writer" problem: Many articles show "Staff Writer" instead of actual author names. This might be accurate, perhaps these articles genuinely didn't have individual bylines, but it's worth investigating whether the actual author names might be buried somewhere in the article text that I'm not capturing.

Entity name normalization: The LLM extracted "NAACP," "Talbot County NAACP," and "NAACP Talbot County" as separate organizations when they're really the same entity. For a beat book, I'd need to do post-processing to normalize these variations into canonical names.

Incomplete people extraction: Even with web search, some people are still being missed—particularly those mentioned without titles or in passing. This suggests I might need additional techniques like named entity recognition to catch everyone.

This assignment taught me that successful entity extraction is as much about understanding your data as it is about crafting good prompts. The web search approach worked because it solved a data problem (incomplete article text in JSON) rather than just a prompt problem. The best prompts in the world can't extract information that isn't there. I also learned that iteration and testing are crucial. Version 1 was good, Version 2 was better, and Version 3 taught me about the value of organization. Each experiment built on insights from the previous one. 

The --limit 5 flag became my best friend—being able to test with just five articles, refine my approach, and test again made the whole process manageable and cost-effective.

**Introduction to Reporting on Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to understand the complex and evolving landscape of this region. The Eastern Shore is a unique area with a rich history, cultural heritage, and diverse communities. This guide provides a comprehensive narrative and chronological story analysis of key events, people, institutions, and issues related to Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore.

**Major Themes**

The stories provided can be organized into several major themes:

1. **Representation and Leadership**: The appointment of Victoria Gomez Lozano to the Governor's Commission on Hispanic Affairs and Jaelon Terrele Moaney to the Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture highlight the importance of representation and leadership in promoting diversity and inclusion.
2. **Community Events and Celebrations**: The Martin Luther King Jr. Breakfast and Banquet, the "In Pursuit of Freedom" lecture, and other events demonstrate the strong sense of community and commitment to celebrating diversity on the Eastern Shore.
3. **Preservation of History and Culture**: The work of the Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture, the Kennard African American Heritage Center, and other organizations emphasizes the importance of preserving the history and culture of African American and Hispanic communities on the Eastern Shore.
4. **Challenges and Opportunities**: The decline of Black watermen on the Eastern Shore and the need for coordinated efforts to preserve Black culture in Maryland highlight the challenges facing these communities, as well as opportunities for growth and development.

**Chronological Story Analysis**

The stories provided can be analyzed chronologically to understand the evolution of coverage on Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore:

* January 14, 2024: Victoria Gomez Lozano is appointed to the Governor's Commission on Hispanic Affairs, highlighting the importance of representation and leadership in promoting diversity and inclusion.
* January 17, 2024: The Martin Luther King Jr. Breakfast and Banquet is held, celebrating the life and legacy of Dr. King and demonstrating the strong sense of community on the Eastern Shore.
* January 31, 2024: Jaelon Terrele Moaney is appointed to the Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture, emphasizing the importance of preserving the history and culture of African American communities on the Eastern Shore.
* February 6, 2024: The "In Pursuit of Freedom" lecture is announced, highlighting the importance of preserving the history and culture of African American communities on the Eastern Shore.
* February 7, 2024: U.S. Sen. Ben Cardin visits with Eastern Shore's Black watermen, discussing ways to publicly memorialize Maryland's Black watermen and designate more towns as heritage sites and ports of memory for the transatlantic slave trade.

**Key People, Institutions, and Issues**

* **Victoria Gomez Lozano**: Appointed to the Governor's Commission on Hispanic Affairs, highlighting the importance of representation and leadership in promoting diversity and inclusion.
* **Jaelon Terrele Moaney**: Appointed to the Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture, emphasizing the importance of preserving the history and culture of African American communities on the Eastern Shore.

* **Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture**: Works to preserve the history and culture of African American communities on the Eastern Shore, including providing grants for historic preservation and cultural programming.
* **Black watermen**: The decline of Black watermen on the Eastern Shore highlights the challenges facing these communities, as well as opportunities for growth and development.

* **Diversity and inclusion**: Emphasize the importance of promoting diversity and inclusion in all aspects of life on the Eastern Shore.
* **Representation and leadership**: Highlight the need for representation and leadership in promoting diversity and inclusion.
* **Preservation of history and culture**: Emphasize the importance of preserving the history and culture of African American and Hispanic communities on the Eastern Shore.
* **Community events and celebrations**: Demonstrate the strong sense of community on the Eastern Shore through events and celebrations.

As a reporter covering Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to stay informed about these key themes, people, institutions, and issues. By using the frequently used words and phrases, you can effectively communicate the complex and evolving landscape of this region to your audience.

=== part1.md ===
**Comprehensive Guide to Covering Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it is essential to understand the evolving landscape of this topic. The Eastern Shore, with its rich history and cultural heritage, presents a unique set of challenges and opportunities for reporting on diversity and inclusion. This guide provides a chronological story analysis, introducing key people, institutions, and issues, and explores major themes rather than presenting an alphabetical list.

**Historical Context and Introduction**

In recent years, the conversation around diversity and inclusion has gained significant traction on the Eastern Shore. This shift is evident in the publication of articles such as "Black travelers want authentic engagement, not checkboxes" (February 9, 2024) by Alana Dillette and Stefanie Benjamin. The article highlights the need for authentic engagement and representation in the travel industry, particularly for Black travelers. This sets the stage for understanding the intricacies of diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore.

**Theme: Authenticity and Representation**

The theme of authenticity and representation is crucial in understanding the evolution of diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore. The article "Black travelers want authentic engagement, not checkboxes" (February 9, 2024) emphasizes the importance of genuine engagement and representation in the travel industry. This theme is further explored in the context of Black History Month, as seen in the article "Unknown history: Black History Month offers opportunity to recognize ‘the people’" (February 16, 2024) by Niambe Davis. The article sheds light on the often-overlooked contributions of African Americans to the region's history and culture.

**Theme: Challenges Facing Black Communities**

Another significant theme is the challenges facing Black communities on the Eastern Shore. The article "Black watermen share about challenges, changes on the Chesapeake" (February 20, 2024) by Annie Hasselgreen delves into the struggles of Black watermen, including government regulations, lack of representation, and racial discrimination. This theme is also reflected in the experiences shared by panelists at the Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum, who highlighted the need for greater support and recognition of Black watermen's contributions to the maritime industry.

**Theme: Faith and Community**

The theme of faith and community is also prominent in the context of diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore. The article "The Creator's act" (February 20, 2024) by Pastor T.D. Nichols emphasizes the importance of recognizing the shared humanity and "one blood" that unites people across different backgrounds. This theme is closely tied to the concept of community and the need for mutual respect, understanding, and celebration of diversity.

**Key People and Institutions**

Several key people and institutions have played a significant role in shaping the conversation around diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore. Notable figures include:

* Alana Dillette and Stefanie Benjamin, authors of "Black travelers want authentic engagement, not checkboxes"
* Niambe Davis, author of "Unknown history: Black History Month offers opportunity to recognize ‘the people’"

Institutions such as the Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum, the St. Michaels Community Conversations on Race, and the American Association of University Women have also contributed to the conversation around diversity and inclusion.

**Comprehensive Guide to Covering Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to understand the complex history and ongoing issues affecting the region's diverse communities. This guide provides a chronological story analysis of key events, introduces crucial people and institutions, and highlights significant themes in the coverage of race and diversity.

**Historical Context and Legacy**

The Eastern Shore has a rich history of diverse communities, including African Americans, Hispanics, and other minority groups. The region's history is marked by significant events, such as the Civil War and the abolitionist movement, which have shaped the current social and cultural landscape.

The story of the US Colored Troops, who fought for their freedom and the Union during the Civil War, is an essential part of the region's history. Chris Pupke, a historian and past president of the Queen Anne's County Historical Society, has worked to share the stories of these brave men and their contributions to the region's history. The proposed memorial to honor the US Colored Troops in Centreville is a testament to the ongoing efforts to recognize and celebrate the region's diverse heritage.

**Education and Community Engagement**

Education and community engagement are critical aspects of promoting diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore. The Frederick Douglass Honor Society, founded by Harriette Lowery and Walter Black, has worked tirelessly to educate the community about the life and legacy of Frederick Douglass, a former slave and prominent abolitionist.

The annual Frederick Douglass Day celebration, which includes a parade, ceremony, and lecture, is an excellent example of community engagement and education. The event brings together people from all walks of life to celebrate the principles of equality and freedom that Douglass espoused.

**Discrimination and Social Justice**

Despite the progress made in promoting diversity and inclusion, discrimination and social justice issues persist on the Eastern Shore. The Maryland State Police's discriminatory hiring practices, which disproportionately affected Black and female applicants, are a disturbing example of the ongoing struggles faced by minority communities.

The $2.75 million settlement approved by the state is a step towards addressing these issues, but more work needs to be done to ensure that all individuals, regardless of race, gender, or national origin, are treated fairly and have equal opportunities.

**LGBTQ+ Issues**

LGBTQ+ issues are also an essential part of the conversation around diversity and inclusion on the Eastern Shore. The Maryland Legislative LGBTQ+ Caucus has been critical of former Governor Larry Hogan's record on LGBTQ+ issues, citing his failure to sign key bills and his lack of proactive support for the community.

The upcoming election and the candidacy of Angela Alsobrooks, who has pledged to support the LGBTQ+ community, have brought attention to the importance of electing leaders who will champion the rights and dignity of all individuals, regardless of their sexual orientation or gender identity.

**Hispanic Heritage Month**

The Eastern Shore's Hispanic community is a vital and growing part of the region's diversity. The annual Hispanic Heritage Festival, hosted by the Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center, celebrates the culture and contributions of Hispanic individuals and families.

The festival, which includes music, food, and activities, is an excellent example of community engagement and cultural exchange. It provides an opportunity for people to come together, learn about different cultures, and celebrate the region's diversity.

**Key People and Institutions**

* Chris Pupke: Historian and past president of the Queen Anne's County Historical Society
* Harriette Lowery: Founding member of the Frederick Douglass Honor Society
* Walter Black: Founding member of the Frederick Douglass Honor Society
* Angela Alsobrooks: Candidate for US Senate and supporter of the LGBTQ+ community
* Larry Hogan: Former Governor of Maryland and Republican nominee for US Senate
* Col. Roland Butler Jr.: Superintendent of the Maryland State Police
* Keidrick Roy: Fellow at Harvard University and author of "American Dark Age: Racial Feudalism and the Rise of Black Liberalism"

* Inclusion

* US Colored Troops

**A Comprehensive Guide to Reporting on Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it is essential to understand the complexities and nuances of this topic. The Eastern Shore has a rich and diverse history, with a mix of cultures, communities, and experiences that shape the region's social fabric. This guide provides a chronological story analysis of the evolution of coverage on this topic, highlighting key people, institutions, and issues that have shaped the narrative over time.

**Historical Context and Early Developments**

The Eastern Shore has a long history of racial tensions and disparities, dating back to the colonial era. However, in recent years, there has been a growing effort to acknowledge and address these issues. One notable example is the lawsuit filed against the town of Federalsburg in 2023, which challenged the town's election practices as violations of the Voting Rights Act. This lawsuit led to a landmark victory for Black voting rights, with the town ultimately adopting a new election system that ensured more representation for Black residents.

**Key Events and Milestones**

* September 2023: The town of Federalsburg holds its first election under the new system, resulting in the election of the town's first two Black council members, Darlene Hammond and Brandy James.
* September 2024: The community comes together to celebrate the first anniversary of the historic election, with events and activities that promote unity and diversity.
* November 2024: The Maryland Lynching Truth and Reconciliation Commission holds a hearing on the region's lynching past, highlighting the need for reconciliation and healing.
* November 2024: The annual American Indian Heritage Day is celebrated at Jefferson Patterson Park and Museum, highlighting the importance of acknowledging and respecting Native American culture and history.

**Major Themes**

1. **Voting Rights and Election Reform**: The struggle for voting rights and election reform has been a major theme in the Eastern Shore, with the lawsuit against Federalsburg being a key example. The adoption of a new election system in Federalsburg has ensured more representation for Black residents, but there is still work to be done to address disparities in other communities.
2. **Racial Reconciliation and Healing**: The Maryland Lynching Truth and Reconciliation Commission's efforts to address the region's lynching past highlight the need for racial reconciliation and healing. This theme is also reflected in the town of Federalsburg's apology and efforts to make amends for past injustices.
3. **Diversity and Inclusion**: The celebration of Native American Heritage Day and the recognition of the importance of diversity and inclusion in communities across the Eastern Shore reflect a growing awareness of the need to acknowledge and respect different cultures and perspectives.

**Key People and Institutions**

* Darlene Hammond and Brandy James: The first two Black council members elected in Federalsburg, who have been instrumental in promoting diversity and inclusion in the town.
* Willie Woods: President of the Caroline County branch of the NAACP, who has been a key advocate for voting rights and election reform.
* The Maryland Lynching Truth and Reconciliation Commission: A state commission established to address the region's lynching past and promote racial reconciliation and healing.
* Jefferson Patterson Park and Museum: A cultural institution that has played a key role in promoting diversity and inclusion through events and activities such as American Indian Heritage Day.

* Voting rights and election reform
* Racial reconciliation and healing
* Social justice and equity
* Native American heritage and culture
* Black community and representation
* Historically Black communities and institutions

**Conclusion**

**Introduction to Reporting on Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to understand the complex and evolving landscape of this topic. The Eastern Shore, a region in Maryland known for its rich history, cultural diversity, and natural beauty, is home to a diverse population with unique experiences and perspectives. In this guide, we'll take a chronological journey through recent stories, highlighting key people, institutions, and issues that have shaped the conversation around race and diversity on the Eastern Shore.

**Theme 1: Identity and Representation**

In February 2025, a proposal to rename Black History Month to African American Heritage Month in Charles County sparked a debate about identity and representation. Commissioner Ralph E. Patterson II, who introduced the proposal, argued that the name change would better reflect the diversity of African American experiences. However, the majority of residents who participated in surveys and meetings opposed the change, citing the importance of preserving the term "Black History Month" as a unifying force for people of African descent from various backgrounds.

This story introduces key figures like Commissioner Patterson and Commissioner Amanda Stewart, who expressed concerns about the potential impact of the name change on different communities. The debate highlights the complexities of representation and identity, showcasing the diverse perspectives within the African American community.

**Theme 2: Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion**

In Talbot County, a resolution to repeal the diversity statement in the employee handbook has stirred controversy. Council Member Dave Stepp, who introduced the resolution, claimed that it was necessary to ensure the county's compliance with federal funding requirements. However, opponents of the resolution argue that it would undermine the county's commitment to diversity, equity, and inclusion.

This story introduces key figures like Council Member Stepp, former Council President Corey Pack, and County Council Vice President Pete Lesher, who expressed concerns about the potential consequences of removing the diversity statement. The debate highlights the importance of diversity, equity, and inclusion in creating a welcoming and inclusive environment for all employees and residents.

**Theme 3: Immigration and Citizenship**

A presentation by Lindsay Waite, a volunteer at the Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center, shed light on the challenges faced by immigrants seeking citizenship. Waite explained the Naturalization process and the importance of providing support and resources for those navigating the system. Gelard Merilus Loulou, a community leader, attended the presentation, citing the concerns of Federalsburg's Haitian community about the potential impact of immigration policies on their lives.

This story introduces key figures like Waite and Merilus Loulou, highlighting the human side of immigration and citizenship. The story showcases the importance of community resources and support for immigrants, as well as the need for inclusive and welcoming policies.

**Theme 4: Community Engagement and Activism**

Letters to the editor, such as those written by Anne C. Stalfort and Peg and Rick Hughes, demonstrate the passion and concern of community members about issues related to diversity, equity, and inclusion. These letters urge the Talbot County Council to maintain the diversity statement and promote a culture of inclusivity and respect.

This story introduces key voices from the community, highlighting the importance of citizen engagement and activism in shaping the conversation around race and diversity. The letters demonstrate the power of community members to hold elected officials accountable and promote positive change.

=== part15.md ===
**Introduction to Reporting on Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, it is essential to understand the complexities and nuances of the region's history, institutions, and community. The Eastern Shore, particularly Talbot County, has a rich and complex history of racial tensions, slavery, and civil rights movements. This guide aims to provide a comprehensive narrative and chronological story analysis of the topic, highlighting key people, institutions, and issues that have shaped the region's discourse on race and diversity.

**Historical Context: The Isaiah Fountain Case and the Legacy of Jim Crow**

The Isaiah Fountain case, which took place in 1919-1921, is a significant example of the region's complex racial history. Fountain, an African American man, was accused of raping a white girl, and his case sparked widespread controversy and side stories. The local press, particularly the Easton Star-Democrat, played a significant role in shaping public opinion and fueling racial tensions. The paper's editor, Samuel E. Shannahan, was a fierce defender of Talbot County's reputation and resisted any criticism of the region's handling of the case.

The Fountain case highlights the region's legacy of Jim Crow laws and racial segregation. The case also demonstrates how the local press can perpetuate or challenge racial stereotypes and biases. The Baltimore newspapers, which covered the case with a more balanced journalistic eye, were seen as "outside" publications that threatened the local narrative.

**The Legacy of Frederick Douglass and the United States Colored Troops**

Frederick Douglass, a native son of Talbot County, was a leading abolitionist, writer, and orator who fought for the rights of African Americans. The United States Colored Troops, which included many men from Talbot County, played a significant role in the Civil War and the fight for freedom and citizenship. The legacy of Douglass and the Colored Troops is still celebrated today, with descendants and community members working to preserve their history and honor their contributions.

The unveiling of a new plaque honoring Douglass at the Talbot County Courthouse is a testament to the ongoing efforts to recognize and celebrate the region's African American heritage. The plaque, which includes the name of Douglass' first wife, Anna Murray, highlights the importance of women's roles in the fight for freedom and equality.

**Contemporary Issues: Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion**

In recent years, the topic of diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) has become a contentious issue in Talbot County. The county's diversity statement, which was implemented in 2020, has been the subject of debate and controversy. Some residents and council members have pushed to repeal the statement, citing concerns about federal funding and compliance with executive orders.

However, many community members have spoken out in support of the diversity statement, arguing that it is essential for promoting inclusivity and respect for all residents. The debate highlights the ongoing challenges of addressing systemic racism and promoting equity in the region.

**Key Themes and Patterns**

Several key themes and patterns emerge from the stories analyzed:

1. **Racial tensions and legacy of Jim Crow**: The region's history of racial segregation and Jim Crow laws continues to shape contemporary discourse on race and diversity.
2. **Importance of preserving African American heritage**: The legacy of Frederick Douglass and the United States Colored Troops highlights the need to preserve and celebrate African American history and culture.
3. **Contemporary debates on DEI**: The debate over the diversity statement in Talbot County reflects the ongoing challenges of addressing systemic racism and promoting equity in the region.
4. **Role of local press and media**: The local press plays a significant role in shaping public opinion and influencing discourse on race and diversity.

**Frequent Used Words and Phrases**

When reporting on race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, some frequent used words and phrases to be aware of include:

* Diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI)
* Systemic racism
* Jim Crow laws
* Racial segregation
* African American heritage
* Frederick Douglass
* United States Colored Troops
* Talbot County
* Eastern Shore
* Local press and media

**Conclusion**

Reporting on race and diversity on the Eastern Shore requires a deep understanding of the region's complex history, institutions, and community. By recognizing key themes and patterns, such as racial tensions, the importance of preserving African American heritage, and contemporary debates on DEI, reporters can provide nuanced and informed coverage of these critical issues. By being aware of frequent used words and phrases, reporters can ensure that their language is respectful and accurate, reflecting the diversity and complexity of the region.

**Comprehensive Guide to Reporting on Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore, it is essential to understand the complexities and nuances of the topic. The region has a rich history, from the Underground Railroad to the present day, and it is crucial to approach the subject with sensitivity and depth. This guide provides a chronological story analysis, introducing key people, institutions, and issues, and is organized by major themes.

**Theme 1: Preserving History and Cultural Heritage**

The Eastern Shore is home to a wealth of historical sites and cultural institutions that showcase the region's diverse heritage. For example, the Adkins Arboretum has partnered with Bloomberg to create guided history walks, which highlight the role of nature in the Underground Railroad (Rooted Wisdom partners with Bloomberg for guided history walk at Adkins Arboretum, February 26, 2025). The Harriet Tubman Museum and Educational Center in Cambridge has also vowed to preserve the legacy of Harriet Tubman, an iconic figure in American history (Harriet Tubman Museum responds to NPS website changes, April 16, 2025).

**Theme 2: Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion**

Diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) initiatives have become a contentious issue in the region, with some council members seeking to repeal existing policies. The Talbot County Council has been at the center of this debate, with Council Member Dave Stepp sponsoring a resolution to repeal the county's diversity statement (Amid a split council and public outcry, vote on Talbot County DEI statement lingers, February 27, 2025). However, many residents and community leaders have pushed back against this effort, arguing that DEI initiatives are essential for creating a more inclusive and equitable society.

**Theme 3: Honoring the Contributions of Black Soldiers**

The Eastern Shore has a significant history of Black soldiers who fought for the Union during the Civil War. A new memorial plaque at the Kennard African American Cultural Heritage Center in Centreville recognizes the contributions of approximately 435 Black soldiers from Queen Anne's County who served in the United States Colored Troops (Plaque honors hundreds of Black Civil War soldiers from Queen Anne's County, May 2, 2025). This effort is part of a broader initiative to acknowledge and honor the often-overlooked contributions of Black soldiers to American history.

**Theme 4: The Intersection of History and Modernity**

The Eastern Shore is a region where history and modernity intersect in complex ways. The story of Ishi, a Native American man who was discovered in 1911 and became a symbol of the clash between traditional and modern ways of life, is a powerful example of this intersection (An age in America comes to an end, May 2, 2025). As the region continues to evolve and grow, it is essential to consider the legacy of the past and its impact on the present.

=== part2.md ===
**A Comprehensive Guide to Reporting on Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, it is essential to understand the complex and evolving landscape of this topic. This guide provides a narrative and chronological analysis of key stories, introducing readers to important people, institutions, and issues.

**Historical Context and Ongoing Struggles**

The Eastern Shore has a rich and complex history, with stories of racial tension and inequality dating back to the late 19th century. The lynching of James Taylor, a 23-year-old Black man, in 1892, is a tragic example of this history. Today, efforts are being made to acknowledge and learn from this past, with initiatives such as the creation of a marker in Taylor's honor and the development of a documentary film, "Get on the Bus," which explores the Civil Rights Movement and its legacy.

**Education and Awareness**

Education and awareness are crucial in addressing issues of race and diversity. The documentary film "Get on the Bus" is an example of how storytelling can be used to educate and raise awareness about the importance of equality and justice. The film follows a group of Eastern Shore residents as they travel to Civil Rights era landmarks, highlighting the ongoing struggle for racial equality and the importance of remembering and learning from the past.

**Hate Crimes and Discrimination**

Hate crimes and discrimination remain a significant concern on the Eastern Shore. A recent example is the case of three 13-year-old students at Plum Point Middle School, who were charged with harassment and hate crimes for displaying swastikas and making Nazi salutes. This incident highlights the need for continued education and awareness, as well as the importance of addressing and preventing hate crimes.

**Voting Rights and Representation**

Voting rights and representation are critical issues on the Eastern Shore, particularly in communities with significant Black populations. The town of Federalsburg recently settled a lawsuit with the NAACP and the Caucus of African American Leaders, agreeing to change its election system to ensure more representation of Black residents. This settlement is a significant step forward, but ongoing efforts are needed to ensure that all communities have equal access to representation and voting rights.

**Key Players and Institutions**

Several key players and institutions are working to address issues of race and diversity on the Eastern Shore. These include:

* The NAACP, which is working to promote voting rights and representation in communities of color
* The Caucus of African American Leaders, which is advocating for policies and initiatives that support Black communities
* The Calvert County State's Attorney's Office, which is working to address hate crimes and discrimination
* The town of Federalsburg, which is implementing changes to its election system and working to promote diversity and inclusion

The increasing presence of ICE (Immigration and Customs Enforcement) in small towns on the Eastern Shore has become a pressing concern for residents, particularly those from Hispanic and immigrant backgrounds. A recent incident in Marydel, a town on the Maryland-Delaware border, illustrates the fear and uncertainty that many families face. The story of Adrian, a 17-year-old whose family members were detained by ICE, highlights the human impact of these enforcement actions. **Historical Context and Ongoing Struggles**

The story of the Frederick C. Malkus Jr. Bridge, named after a state legislator who opposed desegregation, has sparked controversy and calls for its renaming (April 8, 2024). This incident highlights the ongoing struggle to confront and overcome the legacy of racism and segregation on the Eastern Shore. The bridge's namesake, Malkus, was a vocal opponent of civil rights legislation, and his legacy continues to be felt in the community.

In another incident, a historic Black church in Newark, Delaware, was targeted with racist graffiti, forcing the congregation to relocate its Easter services (April 9, 2024). This hate crime is a stark reminder of the ongoing struggles faced by African American communities in the region.

**Community Engagement and Activism**

In response to these incidents, community leaders and organizations have come together to advocate for change. The Caucus of African American Leaders, led by Carl Snowden, has been instrumental in pushing for the renaming of the Malkus Bridge and other monuments to controversial figures (April 8, 2024). The group has also met with state officials, including Governor Wes Moore, to discuss these issues.

The U.S. Department of Justice has also engaged with the community, hosting a conversation on civil rights issues and listening to concerns from local residents (April 10, 2024). This event was part of a larger effort to build trust and partnership between law enforcement and the community.

**Anniversaries and Reflections**

The 60th anniversary of the Civil Rights Act has provided an opportunity for reflection on the progress made and the challenges that still exist (April 23, 2024). **Common Themes and Patterns**

=== part4.md ===
**Comprehensive Guide to Reporting on Race and Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering the intersection of race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to understand the historical context, key players, and institutions that have shaped this complex narrative. This guide provides a chronological story analysis, highlighting major themes, key events, and notable figures that have influenced the conversation on race and diversity in the region.

**Historical Context: The Legacy of Slavery and Segregation**

The story of The Pearl, a thwarted slave escape from Washington, D.C. in 1848, sets the stage for understanding the region's tumultuous history with slavery and segregation. The attempted escape, led by Daniel Bell and Capt. Daniel Drayton, was ultimately foiled, and the 77 enslaved individuals were returned to their owners. This event, commemorated annually by Westminster Presbyterian Church, serves as a poignant reminder of the region's past and the ongoing struggle for racial equality.

**The Civil Rights Movement and its Legacy**

The civil rights movement of the 1960s had a profound impact on the Eastern Shore, with figures like Gloria Richardson, a leader of the Cambridge Movement, fighting for desegregation and economic opportunities for Black people. The Treaty of Cambridge, negotiated by Richardson and Attorney General Robert Kennedy, marked a significant milestone in the struggle for civil rights. However, this progress was often met with resistance, as exemplified by the actions of Sen. Frederick C. Malkus Jr., a staunch segregationist who opposed civil rights legislation.

**Contemporary Issues: Renaming the Malkus Bridge and Addressing Racism**

The debate over renaming the Frederick C. Malkus Jr. Bridge, currently named after the segregationist senator, has sparked a conversation about the legacy of racism and the importance of representation. The Alpha Kappa Alpha Sorority and the Caucus of African American Leaders have called for the bridge to be renamed in honor of Gloria Richardson, citing Malkus' history of opposing civil rights. This effort has been met with resistance from some community members, highlighting the ongoing tension between those who seek to confront the region's racist past and those who prefer to maintain the status quo.

**Racial Slurs and Online Harassment: A Persistent Problem**

The disruption of a Newark City Council meeting by online provocateurs yelling racial slurs has brought attention to the ongoing issue of racism and online harassment. This incident, which prompted an investigation by local police, underscores the need for greater awareness and action to address hate speech and promote inclusive, respectful dialogue.

**Institutions and Initiatives: Promoting Equity and Inclusion**

The Southern Maryland Equity in History Coalition, launched in May 2024, aims to promote a more inclusive understanding of the region's history, highlighting the experiences of Black, Indigenous, and other people of color. This initiative, supported by local organizations and institutions, reflects a growing recognition of the importance of equity and diversity in shaping the narrative of the Eastern Shore.

**Frequent Terms and Concepts**

* **Desegregation**: The process of ending racial segregation, particularly in public facilities, education, and employment.
* **Racial justice**: The pursuit of equal treatment and opportunities for all individuals, regardless of race or ethnicity.
* **Institutional racism**: The perpetuation of racist policies and practices within institutions, such as schools, government agencies, and businesses.
* **Equity**: The principle of fairness and justice, ensuring that all individuals have access to the same opportunities and resources.
* **Inclusion**: The practice of creating environments that welcome and value diverse perspectives, experiences, and identities.
* **Systemic racism**: The perpetuation of racist policies and practices within systems, such as education, housing, and employment, which can have a disproportionate impact on marginalized communities.

As a reporter covering the intersection of race and diversity on the Eastern Shore, it's essential to be familiar with these concepts and terms, as well as the historical context and key players that have shaped the region's narrative. By doing so, you'll be better equipped to tell the stories that need to be told, promoting greater understanding, empathy, and action towards a more equitable and inclusive future.

**Introduction to Reporting on Race & Diversity on the Eastern Shore**

As a reporter covering the Eastern Shore, understanding the complexities of race and diversity is crucial in providing comprehensive and nuanced coverage of the region. The Eastern Shore, with its rich history and cultural heritage, offers a unique perspective on the issues of race and diversity. This guide aims to provide a narrative and chronological analysis of key stories, themes, and issues, as well as introduce key people, institutions, and concerns that are essential to reporting on this topic.

**Major Themes**

1. **Celebrations and Commemorations**: The Eastern Shore is home to various celebrations and commemorations that highlight the region's diverse cultural heritage. For example, the Juneteenth Celebration at the Academy Art Museum, which honors the emancipation of enslaved African Americans, features local fashion exhibitors, musical performances, and a free outdoor block party. The ceremony honoring 18 Black Union soldiers buried in the Unionville cemetery is another testament to the region's commitment to preserving its history and cultural identity.
2. **Community Activism and Collective Power**: The region has a strong tradition of community activism, with organizations and individuals working together to effect change. The Caucus of African American Leaders, led by Carl Snowden, is a prominent example of collective power in action. The caucus has been instrumental in promoting civil rights and social justice, and its meetings often feature discussions on issues such as police brutality and community empowerment.
3. **Inclusion and Diversity**: The Eastern Shore is also home to a growing LGBTQ+ community, with events like the Caroline Pride Festival promoting diversity, inclusion, and respect. The festival's motto, "Diversity Creates Community," reflects the region's aspiration to create a welcoming and inclusive environment for all individuals, regardless of their background, identity, or orientation.
4. **Challenges and Controversies**: Despite the progress made in promoting diversity and inclusion, the region still faces challenges and controversies. The opposition to Pride events and recognition in Easton, led by Council President Frank Gunsallus and Council Member David Montgomery, highlights the need for ongoing discussion and education on issues of diversity and inclusion.

**Key People and Institutions**

* **Carl Snowden**: Convener of the Caucus of African American Leaders, Snowden is a prominent voice on civil rights and social justice issues.
* **Angel Perez**: Founder of the Caroline Pride Festival, Perez is a leading advocate for LGBTQ+ rights and inclusion.
* **The Academy Art Museum**: The museum has been a hub for cultural events and exhibitions, including the Juneteenth Celebration.
* **The Caucus of African American Leaders**: A coalition of community leaders and organizations working to promote civil rights and social justice.
* **The Caroline Pride Festival**: An annual event celebrating diversity, inclusion, and respect for the LGBTQ+ community.

**Chronological Analysis**

* **May 23, 2024**: The Academy Art Museum announces its 13th Annual Juneteenth Celebration, featuring local fashion exhibitors and musical performances.
* **May 26, 2024**: A ceremony is held to honor 18 Black Union soldiers buried in the Unionville cemetery, highlighting the region's rich history and cultural heritage.
* **June 5, 2024**: The Caucus of African American Leaders holds a meeting in Cambridge, featuring discussions on civil rights and social justice issues.
* **June 5, 2024**: The Caroline Pride Festival is held in Denton, promoting diversity, inclusion, and respect for the LGBTQ+ community.
* **June 9, 2024**: Council President Frank Gunsallus and Council Member David Montgomery face opposition for their comments on Pride events and recognition in Easton.

**Frequently Used Words and Terms**

* **Diversity**: The presence of different groups or individuals with unique characteristics, experiences, and perspectives.
* **Inclusion**: The act of creating an environment where all individuals feel welcome, valued, and respected.
* **Collective power**: The idea that individuals and groups can come together to effect change and promote social justice.
* **Civil rights**: The rights and freedoms guaranteed to individuals by law, including the right to equality and freedom from discrimination.
* **Social justice**: The pursuit of fairness, equality, and human rights for all individuals, particularly those who have been marginalized or oppressed.
* **LGBTQ+**: An abbreviation for lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, and queer, referring to individuals who identify as part of the LGBTQ+ community.
* **Intersectionality**: The idea that individuals have multiple identities and experiences that intersect and interact to shape their perspectives and experiences.

**Reporting Tips**

* **Be respectful and inclusive**: Use language and terminology that is respectful and inclusive of all individuals and groups.
* **Listen to diverse perspectives**: Seek out and listen to the perspectives and experiences of individuals from diverse backgrounds and identities.
* **Provide context**: Provide historical and cultural context to help readers understand the complexities of issues related to race and diversity.
* **Avoid assumptions**: Avoid making assumptions about individuals or groups based on their appearance, identity, or background.
* **Seek out experts**: Seek out experts and individuals with experience and knowledge on issues related to race and diversity.

By following these tips and staying informed about key issues and developments, reporters can provide comprehensive and nuanced coverage of race and diversity on the Eastern Shore.

The debate around the LGBTQ+ community and Pride events is not without controversy.
