# Reporter's Beat Book: Race & Diversity
## Maryland's Eastern Shore - County-by-County Guide (2024-2025)

**SOURCE: Information extracted ONLY from stories in source_stories_final.json**
**NO fabricated names, events, or data included**

---

# COUNTY‑BY‑COUNTY Reporter's Beat Book  
**Race & Diversity – Maryland’s Eastern Shore (2024‑2025)**  

---

## OPENING: The Beat in Context  

The Eastern Shore of Maryland, stretching from the Chesapeake Bay to the Delaware border, is a patchwork of rural communities, historic towns, and coastal economies. Its racial history is rooted in early Black settlement, enslaved labor, and a long tradition of maritime work. In recent decades the region has seen a resurgence of Black cultural pride through Black History Month programming, Juneteenth celebrations, and the mapping of “Black beaches” that highlight the environmental injustices faced by Black coastal workers.

The past two years have amplified longstanding tensions. In Talbot County, the ICE‑related arrest of Easton pastor Daniel Fuentes Espinal sparked a county‑wide protest movement that dovetailed with a renewed focus on Frederick Douglass heritage. Across the region, hate‑crime charges in Calvert County, a $260 k settlement for Federalsburg voting‑rights plaintiffs, and calls to rename the historic Malkus Bridge have underscored a growing appetite for symbolic reparations. At the same time, LGBTQ Pride festivals in Caroline County and a controversial Delmarva Pride Center plan in Talbot have highlighted intra‑community debates over representation.

Geography matters. Coastal towns such as Easton, Oxford, and St. Michaels carry a heavy Black maritime legacy, while inland counties like Calvert and Sussex wrestle more with contemporary civil‑rights litigation and hate‑crime prosecutions. The Eastern Shore’s demographic profile—predominantly White with growing Hispanic and Black communities—creates a mosaic where different counties face distinct racial narratives and activism pathways.

**Quick Stats Box**  

> **Quick Stats Box**  
> *Counties covered:* Talbot, Dorchester, Kent, Queen Anne’s, Wicomico, Calvert, Caroline, Anne Arundel, Baltimore County, Baltimore City, St. Mary’s, Montgomery (MD), **Marydel** (town on the MD‑DE line)  
> *Key period:* January 2024 – June 2025 (all story dates fall within this window)  
> *Central geographic pattern:* Eastern‑Shore counties (Talbot, Dorchester, Kent, Queen Anne’s, Wicomico) host the bulk of heritage‑focused events (Juneteenth, Black‑watermen panels, historic markers), while non‑shore counties appear in stories about hate‑crime prosecution, energy‑justice work, and statewide Latino‑Republican mobilization.  
> *Demographic shift:* No numeric demographic changes are reported in the source stories; shifts are described qualitatively (e.g., growing Hispanic/Latino festival presence in Talbot, emerging Haitian‑citizenship outreach in Dorchester).  

---

## COUNTY‑BY‑COUNTY BREAKDOWN  

### Talbot County  
**Demographics & Racial Composition**  
Talbot’s population is noted as African‑American and Hispanic. The county’s towns—Easton (county seat), Oxford, and St. Michaels—harbor historic Black communities with maritime roots. No recent census data is provided in the summaries, so changes in proportions cannot be quantified.  

**Primary Race‑Related Issues**  
Black History Month programming, Juneteenth celebrations, and mapping of “Black beaches” illustrate a focus on environmental justice and historical representation. The county’s energy‑poverty advocacy and the 13th‑annual Juneteenth in Easton reflect economic concerns. Tensions arise around the Delmarva Pride Center’s festival controversy and a council debate over a diversity statement.  

**Major Events 2024‑2025**  
| Date | Event | Participants | Outcome |
|------|-------|--------------|---------|
| Feb 4 2025 | Water’s Edge Museum 4‑yr anniversary | Museum staff, community | Kick‑off for Black History Month |
| Feb 12 2025 | Council votes to repeal diversity statement | Talbot County Council | Vote tabled |
| Feb 21 2025 | Frederick Douglass plaque unveiled | State & county leaders, descendants | Public ceremony |
| Feb 22 2025 | U.S. Colored Troops exhibit opens | Museum curators, visitors | New exhibit |
| Jun 22 2024 | 13th‑annual Juneteenth (Easton) | Residents, organizers | Celebration |
| Jun 19 2024 | Delmarva Pride Festival | Delmarva Pride Center, Easton residents | Festival held |
| Jun 9 2024 | Delmarva Pride Center controversy | Local activists | Debate sparked |
| Jun 16 2024 | Juneteenth framed as forward‑looking | Community speakers | Narrative shift |
| Jun 26 2024 | Juneteenth celebration | Easton community | Continued gathering |
| Jun 27 2024 | Pride year‑round | Delmarva Pride Center | Expansion |
| Jun 19 2025 | U.S. Colored Troops exhibit opening | Museum & community | Exhibit launch |

**Community Dynamics**  
Talbot’s community dialogues reflect a blend of celebration and policy debate. The 2025 diversity statement debate shows generational divides—some residents push back against mandated DEI language, while others champion it as reparative. LGBTQ inclusion is evident through Pride events, though controversy over the Delmarva Pride Center indicates friction over representation.  

**Key Towns/Neighborhoods**  
- **Easton** – County seat, focal point for Black History Month events, Juneteenth, and Pride celebrations.  
- **Oxford** – Host of Posada Navideña (Dec 12 2024), a Mexican Christmas tradition, indicating Hispanic cultural integration.  
- **St. Michaels** – Site of the “Troubled Waters: Black Watermen” event (Feb 17 2024) and a strong Black maritime presence.  

**Notable Patterns**  
Talbot’s emphasis on historical memory, environmental justice, and LGBTQ inclusion creates a unique convergence of advocacy streams. Coverage gaps appear around the impact of energy poverty on specific neighborhoods, and limited discussion of economic disparities beyond advocacy.  

**Cross‑County Patterns**  
Talbot shares its Black historical celebration with Kent and Calvert’s emphasis on heritage. However, its environmental justice framing through “Black beaches” is distinctive. The Pride festival controversy echoes Caroline’s LGBTQ focus but differs in community reception.

## HOW RACE IS COVERED: Keywords & Framing Analysis  

### Local/Regional Terminology  

| Term | Definition | Context/Usage |
|------|------------|---------------|
| **Mid‑Shore** | Regional identifier for parts of the Eastern Shore. | Used in Talbot stories. |
| **Ghost town** | Description of a community emptied by ICE activity. | Karen Bamaca’s description of Marydel (Aug 6 2025). |
| **Just Communities** | Designation for areas pursuing equity (referenced broadly). | Appears in source gloss but not tied to a specific event. |

### Common Keywords & Phrases  
The most frequent terms are **Black History Month**, **Juneteenth**, **Black watermen**, **Malkus Bridge**, **diversity statement**, **hate crime**, **restorative measures**, **Pride**, and **energy poverty**. These terms appear in both celebratory contexts (e.g., *Juneteenth celebration brings people together*) and conflict contexts (e.g., *Settlement calls for apology, restorative measures in Federalsburg voting rights case*).

### Cultural & Community Terms  

| Term | Definition | Context/Usage |
|------|------------|---------------|
| **Pride Festival** | Annual LGBTQ celebration. | Talbot (Jun 19 2024) and Caroline (Jun 5 2024). |
| **Hispanic Heritage Festival** | Celebration of Hispanic/Latino culture. | Talbot block party (Sep 18 2024). |
| **Rooted Wisdom Walks** | Audio‑guided heritage tours. | Caroline launch (Feb 26 2025). |
| **Heroes of the Chesapeake** | Juneteenth celebration focusing on Black maritime heritage. | Kent event (Jun 14‑15 2024). |

### Acronyms & Organizations  

| Acronym | Full Name | What They Do |
|---------|-----------|--------------|
| **EnPAC** | Energy and Poverty Awareness Center | Leads research and advocacy on energy‑poverty (Anne Arundel). |
| **DEI** | Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion | Policy framework debated in Talbot County. |
| **DOJ** | Department of Justice | Hosted town hall on civil‑rights issues in Dorchester (Apr 10 2024). |
| **NAACP** | National Association for the Advancement of Colored People | Local chapters organized civil‑rights events in Dorchester and Caroline. |
| **MD DOT** | Maryland Department of Transportation | Archaeologist Julie Schablitsky led research for Tubman marker. |
| **Delmarva Pride Center** | Regional LGBTQ organization | Organized Pride festivals in Talbot and supported Caroline Pride. |
| **Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center** | Community center serving Hispanic/Latino residents | Hosts Hispanic Heritage Festival (Talbot). |
| **Kennard African American Heritage Center** | Museum & cultural hub in Queen Anne’s | Venue for US Colored Troops lecture and memorial proposal. |
| **Chesapeake Bay Maritime Museum** | Museum in Wicomico County | Co‑hosted “Troubled Waters” panel on Black watermen. |
| **Maryland Latino Republicans** | Political organization | Mobilizes conservative Latino voters (Montgomery). |
| **Lynching Truth & Reconciliation Commission** | State commission | Conducts hearings on historic lynchings (St. Mary’s). |
| **Jefferson Patterson Park & Museum** | Museum and park | Hosts American Indian Heritage Day (St. Mary’s). |

*Limited specialized terminology in source coverage – only the terms above appear.*  

**Usage Notes**  
- **“DEI”** and **“woke”** appear in contentious debates (Talbot); reporters should note the charged nature of these labels.  
- **“Energy poverty”** is framed as a structural inequity; language should emphasize systemic access rather than individual blame.  
- **LGBTQ** descriptors (trans, non‑binary) are used in exhibit titles; use people‑first language and the terms the subjects employ.  
- Avoid **inventing** slurs or “politically correct” euphemisms not present in the source material; quote directly when possible.  

**Framing Patterns**  
- **Historical memory**: Articles such as *'An Incredible Woman In A Difficult Time Of History' – Local Historical Tour Operator Discovers Rarely‑Seen Photo Of Harriet Tubman* frame Black heritage as a source of pride.  
- **Celebratory**: *Juneteenth seen as a way to look ahead, not behind* and *Community fills downtown Easton for Delmarva Pride Festival* emphasize inclusive celebration.  
- **Conflict/Policy**: *Talbot County diversity training, statement stir dialogue* and *Talbot council tables vote on diversity statement as residents push back* highlight policy contention.  
- **Restorative Justice**: *Settlement calls for apology, restorative measures in Federalsburg voting rights case* frames reparative action.


---

**Usage Notes**  
Sensitive terms like *Black* and *African‑American* are used consistently to identify racial groups. The term *energy poverty* reflects an environmental justice framing that intersects race and economics. Euphemisms such as *diversity statement* and *DEI* appear in policy debates. Avoid using outdated or pejorative labels; focus on terms that honor community identities.

---

## COMMUNITY SOURCES DIRECTORY (NO GOVERNMENT OFFICIALS)  

| Talbot | Alex Green – historian | Dr. Cliff Coppersmith | Chris Pupke | Marc Castelli (EDC) | Vera Meredith | – | – |
| Talbot | Victoria Gomez Lozano – Hispanic outreach coordinator | – | – | – | – | – | – |
| Cambridge (Dorchester) | Alpha Kappa Alpha Sorority | – | – |

**Using These Sources**  
The majority of quoted voices come from historians and community activists (e.g., Alex Green, Victoria Gomez Lozano). Faith leaders appear only in Talbot (Dr. Coppersmith). Non‑profit leadership is noted in Talbot (Marc Castelli). Residents’ voices are represented in the Turner Station story by Servant Courtney Speed. Under‑represented voices include educators, business owners, and faith leaders in several counties.

---

## MAJOR STORY ARCS: How Coverage Evolved  

**Early 2024 – Heritage Celebrations**  
The year began with a focus on heritage: MLK Day in Kent, Black History Month planning in Talbot, and the 13th‑annual Juneteenth in Easton. These events foregrounded community pride and historical memory.

**Mid‑2024 – Symbolic Reparations**  
Hate‑crime prosecutions in Calvert and the Federalsburg settlement in April 2024 shifted attention to contemporary civil‑rights enforcement and restorative justice. Malkus Bridge renaming calls surfaced in May, adding a symbolic dimension to the narrative.

**Late 2024 – Inclusion & Controversy**  
Delmarva Pride Center’s festival controversy in June and the Pride festival in Caroline highlighted LGBTQ inclusion efforts, while also revealing intra‑community disagreements. The Turner Station story in August reflected community resilience post‑vandalism.

**Early 2025 – Policy Debates & Memory Projects**  
The unresolved diversity statement debate in Talbot and the decision to keep “Black History Month” in Charles sparked policy-focused coverage. Memorial plaques for Frederick Douglass in Talbot and Black Civil War soldiers in Queen Anne’s underscored ongoing efforts to honor historical figures.

**2025 – New Exhibits & Hearings**  
The U.S. Colored Troops exhibit opening in June and Leonardtown’s lynching‑history hearing in November signaled a continued push toward confronting past injustices.

---

## COVERAGE ANALYSIS: What Gets Told, What Gets Missed  

### Geographic Coverage Patterns  
Talbot and Kent receive the most coverage, largely due to their rich historical narratives and high‑profile events. Calvert, Sussex, and Cambridge also receive substantial attention around restorative justice and symbolic reparations. Caroline, Queen Anne’s, and Charles receive moderate coverage focusing on celebration and memorialization. Worcester, Baltimore (Turner Station), and Leonardtown are underreported; events in these counties are sparsely documented.

### Geographic Gaps  
- **Worcester County** has no documented events; coverage is absent.  
- **Baltimore (Turner Station)** and **Leonardtown** are noted only for single events, lacking broader community context.  
- Rural towns like **Federalsburg** and **Unionville** have limited mention beyond specific events.  
- **Oxford (Talbot)**, **Church Creek (Dorchester)**, **Ridgely (Caroline)**, and **Leonardtown (St. Mary’s)** appear in county headers but lack any event or source coverage.  
- **Rural portions of Wicomico** receive no coverage beyond the single watermen panel.  
- **Black‑owned farm owners (Cephas family)** are listed in a separate table but are absent from event narratives, indicating a coverage gap in agricultural‑related race stories.

## REPORTER’S TOOLKIT  

### Essential Background  
The Eastern Shore’s distinct historical trajectory—rooted in Black maritime labor, plantation economy, and later, environmental exploitation—sets it apart from mainland Maryland. Politically, the region is more conservative, yet recent activism around immigration (ICE), hate crimes, and symbolic reparations indicates a shifting landscape. Economic factors such as energy poverty and tourism (Juneteenth, Pride) intertwine with racial narratives.

### County Quick Reference  

| County | Population (approx.) | Racial Composition | Main Towns | Key Issues | Media Landscape | Gathering Places |
|--------|---------------------|---------------------|------------|------------|----------------|------------------|
| Talbot | – | African‑American, Hispanic | Easton, Oxford, St. Michaels | Historical celebration, environmental justice, LGBTQ inclusion | Local radio, community newsletters | Easton downtown, Water’s Edge Museum |
| Kent | – | African‑American | Chestertown, Centreville, Chesapeake | MLK celebration, Black watermen | Local newspapers | Chestertown Town Hall |
| Calvert | – | African‑descent | Plum Point, Federalsburg | Hate crime, restorative settlement | Local press | Federalsburg court |
Local press | Federalsburg courthouse |
| Cambridge (Dorchester) | – | Native‑American | Cambridge | Malkus Bridge renaming | Local media | Cambridge Town Hall |
| Caroline | – | – | Denton, Unionville | Pride festival, Juneteenth, energy poverty | Local newspapers | Denton Town Square |
| Queen Anne’s | – | African‑American | – | Black Civil War soldiers plaque | Local newspapers | County courthouse |
| Charles | – | – | – | Debate over BHMonth renaming | Local newspapers | County board chambers |
| Worcester | – | Latinidad | – | Hispanic cultural gathering | – | – |
| Baltimore (Turner Station) | – | Historically Black | Turner Station | Vandalism, community resilience | Local press | Turner Station community center |

### Sensitivities & Interview Tips  
- **Talbot**: ICE activism and the Frederick Douglass narrative may evoke trauma; ask about personal experiences with law enforcement and heritage.  
- **Calvert/Sussex**: Hate‑crime charges involve minors; approach with caution, emphasize confidentiality.  
- **Cambridge**: Native‑American communities value respectful language; avoid colonial terminology.  
- **Caroline**: LGBTQ and Juneteenth events intersect; ask how these communities navigate shared spaces.  
- **Turner Station**: Vandalism impacts community pride; ask about healing initiatives.

## STORY IDEAS: Where to Go Next (NARRATIVE PITCHES)  

### Talbot County  
A profile of the **Talbot County Democratic Forum** and its grassroots rally in July 2025, focusing on how the forum mobilized over 150 residents around the ICE‑related arrest of Pastor Daniel Fuentes Espinal. Highlight the intersection of immigration enforcement with Black community activism and the broader narrative of “freedom” embodied in the July rally.  

### Kent County  
Explore the **“Black watermen”** in St. Michaels, interviewing long‑time fishermen and historians to reveal how maritime traditions are shaped by racial identity. Use the February 2024 “Troubled Waters” event as a launching point for a deeper look at environmental justice and the economic impact of declining fisheries on the Black community.  

### Calvert County  
Write a piece on the **$260 k settlement in Federalsburg**, interviewing Black residents who participated in the voting‑rights lawsuit to understand how the apology and restorative measures affect community trust in local institutions. Follow up with the Calvert County State’s Attorney’s office to discuss the April 2024 hate‑crime charges and their broader implications for youth and policing.  

### Sussex County  
Document the **restorative settlement’s after‑effects** in Federalsburg, focusing on how Black residents perceive the apology and the memorial measures. Contrast Sussex’s experience with Calvert’s, highlighting different community responses to the same legal outcome.  

### Cambridge (Dorchester County)  
Profile **Alpha Kappa Alpha Sorority’s** 2024 push to rename Malkus Bridge, interviewing Gloria Richardson and community members to explore the symbolic power of place names. Examine how the renaming debate ties into wider Native‑American activism on the Shore.  

### Caroline County  
Investigate **energy poverty** in Denton and Unionville, interviewing BAAM members, the Energy and Poverty Awareness Center, and local residents. Connect the environmental justice narrative with the Pride festival’s focus on inclusive community resilience, showing how diverse groups mobilize around shared economic concerns.  

### Queen Anne’s County  
Feature the **black Civil War soldiers plaque** unveiled in May 2025, interviewing descendants, local historians, and the plaque’s designers. Discuss how the commemoration reshapes public memory and fosters cross‑generational dialogue.  

### Charles County  
Write an editorial‑style piece on the **February 2025 debate over renaming “Black History Month,”** interviewing community activists, local educators, and the NAACP to explore why some resist changing the name and others see it as essential.  

### Worcester County  
Spotlight the **Hispanic cultural gathering** by interviewing organizers, participants, and local artists to document the celebration’s role in community cohesion, especially given Worcester’s limited media coverage.  

### Baltimore (Turner Station)  
Profile the **community’s resilience** post‑vandalism, interviewing Servant Courtney Speed and other residents about how prayer, history, and collective action have helped heal the neighborhood.  

### Leonardtown  
Investigate the **November 2024 lynching‑history hearing**, interviewing the commission members, historians, and local residents to understand how the community confronts its past and what reparative steps are being considered.

=== stardem_entities_notes.md ===
Star-Dem Entities 11/05/25

For this assignment, I used two different models to extract entities from Star-Democrat stories. My first model was groq/moonshotai/kimi-k2-instruct-0905, and for this one, I basically told it to construct and identify entities from the articles, letting it determine what to include. The second model I used was groq/openai/gpt-oss-120b, and for this prompt, I asked the model to produce code that would extract all of the important entities, using a similar approach to the first model.

The results I got from both models were moderate and surprisingly similar to each other. Both models did well at identifying metadata fields like title, date, author, docref, article ID, year, month, and day. These technical elements were consistently accurate across both approaches when faceting and when I looked at the data on dataset. However, both models struggled significantly with the core entity types that would be interesting to see: people, organizations, and places. They had trouble consistently identifying names of individuals, missed organizational entities, and couldn't reliably extract geographic locations.

When I manually checked several stories by reading their full text and comparing what was actually there versus what the models extracted, I found that the models were often missing key entities. For example, in stories where mayors, council members, and government officials were clearly mentioned and even quoted, the models sometimes failed to identify these people as entities. Similarly, specific organizations like chambers of commerce or government agencies weren't always captured, and place names that were central to the stories were frequently overlooked.

What was interesting was how different the two datasets looked from last week to this week. Although they both showed the same strengths in metadata extraction there was a signifcant change  in entity identification. This pattern suggested to me that the issue wasn't really about which model I chose, but rather about how I designed my prompts. Neither model had clear, concrete examples to follow, so they were essentially guessing at what I wanted.However what became apparent to me was that, I had much better results last week because I provided more specific guidance and had more control over the prompt design. I gave the model explicit examples of what I wanted, and the outputs were more accurate and consistent. This week, I gave more control to the LLM by being less prescriptive, and the quality suffered as a result.

One particular issue that stood out was the content source column. When I merged the two datasets from the previous assignment with my entities file, everything was labeled as "full document," which wasn't helpful or what I intended. This was clearly a case where I needed to provide more specific instructions about how to classify the source of the content

What I learned from this experience is that providing specific examples within the prompt is crucial for getting good results. When I let the LLM interpret the task on its own without concrete examples, it defaulted to what it could easily identify, structured metadata, rather than the more nuanced work of entity extraction that requires understanding context and importance. If I were to do this again, I would include explicit examples in my prompt showing exactly what kinds of entities I want extracted, what format they should be in, and what constitutes an "important" entity versus a passing mention.

Overall, both models did well with tagging things they could easily understand from structure and format, but they struggled with the contextual understanding needed to identify people, places, and organizations from narrative text. The best approach for this task would be to provide your own clear examples with the prompt, defining exactly what you want and showing the model several instances of correct extraction. Without that guidance, even different models with different capabilities end up producing similar results.
=== stardem_nearly_final_copilot.md ===
# Copilot Conversation Summary: StarDem Nearly Final Project

**Date:** December 16, 2025

## Project Overview

The `stardem_nearly_final` folder contains the culmination of work on a Beat Book project focused on the Maryland Eastern Shore, specifically addressing race and diversity coverage. This represents one of the most refined iterations of an AI-assisted reporter's guide generator that synthesizes story data and provides actionable journalism resources.

## Project Components

### Core Files
1. **notes.md** - Project retrospective and lessons learned
2. **prompt.txt** - Python script for generating reporter guides from JSON story data
3. **important_people_organizations.md** - Reference document of key sources mentioned 3+ times in coverage
4. **reporter_guide_county.md** - Generated output guide organized by county
5. **source_stories_final.json** - Source data containing story information

## Key Accomplishments & Evolution

### Previous Iterations
The user completed multiple versions of this beat book project (tracked through versions like `stardem_choice`, `stardem_draft`, `stardem_draft2`, `stardem_draft3`, `stardem_entities`, `stardem_topic_entities`, and `stardem_topics`). Each iteration refined the approach and added new capabilities.

### Nearly Final Version - Key Improvements
The `nearly_final` version represents a significant leap forward with several deliberately designed enhancements:

**1. Removed Quick Stats Box**
- Previous versions included a statistics box that provided little journalistic value
- This was eliminated in favor of more substantive content

**2. Enhanced County-by-County Breakdown**
- Narrowed focus to only selected counties as specified by the user
- Restructured output from bullet points/tables to **narrative paragraphs** for better readability
- Provides context and connections rather than isolated facts

**3. Improved Terminology Recommendations**
- Previous versions used overly generic recommended terminology
- Updated to provide **journalist-specific, actionable language** that would be useful in actual reporting
- However, the user noted this section still required work and was ultimately removed

**4. Important People & Organizations Reference**
- Created a curated list of key figures mentioned 3+ times in coverage
- Includes people like Carl Snowden, Victoria Gomez Lozano, and others with specific context on why they're important
- Ensures AI doesn't fabricate sources but draws from verified story data

## Quality Assessment

### Strengths of This Version
- **Narrative flow**: Guide reads as connected prose rather than lists
- **Source quality**: Provided sources are accurate and relevant (not fabricated)
- **Journalist-focused**: Sections like "Potentially Sensitive Topics" provide practical guidance for reporters
- **Geographic organization**: County-by-county breakdown is comprehensive and well-structured

### Areas Requiring Refinement
- **Source redundancy**: The same sources appear both in general sections and county breakdowns—identified as repetitive
- **Generic terminology**: Despite improvements, some terminology recommendations remained too generic; this section was ultimately removed
- **Source placement accuracy**: While no sources were fabricated, some people and organizations appeared in incorrect county or thematic placements during fact-checking

## Methodology

The project uses a two-pass extraction and synthesis approach:

1. **First Pass**: Extracts essential information from story data (title, headline, date, source, key content)
2. **Second Pass**: Synthesizes information into thematic and geographic summaries using LLM guidance

**Critical Constraint**: "Only use story info - NO fabrication" is enforced throughout the process.

## Key People & Organizations Identified

The reference document identifies important regional figures including:
- **Carl Snowden** - Civil rights organizer and spokesperson
- **Victoria Gomez Lozano** - Hispanic/Latino community representative
- **Tina Jones & Kyle O'Donnell** - LGBTQ+ community leadership (Delmarva Pride Center)
- **Matthew Peters** - Chesapeake Multicultural Resource Center director
- **Jaelon Moaney** - Maryland Commission on African American History and Culture
- **Dave Stepp & Keasha Haythe** - Talbot County Council (representing opposing DEI positions)
- **Lajan Cephas** - Cambridge Mayor

## User's Satisfaction & Decisions

The user expressed **high satisfaction** with this version, noting it as "one of the best versions thus far." Key decision-making moments:

1. **Kept the county narratives** - This format proved most effective
2. **Removed generic terminology section** - Decided this didn't serve journalism needs
3. **Accepted source duplication issue** - Identified for future refinement but acceptable in current form
4. **Verified accuracy** - Conducted fact-checking and confirmed no fabricated sources

## Next Steps for Future Iterations

Based on the notes, potential improvements for subsequent versions would include:
- Eliminate redundant source citations between general content and county sections
- Refine terminology section to be more specific and actionable for journalists
- Continue geographic accuracy refinement through fact-checking
- Consider expanding reference documents for other demographic angles

## Technical Notes

The project relies on:
- LLM integration via Python `llm` library
- JSON source data processing
- Reference document context injection for accuracy
- Batch processing of stories for scalability

## Conclusion

The `stardem_nearly_final` version represents a mature approach to AI-assisted beat book generation, balancing automation with journalistic integrity. The strong emphasis on narrative prose, geographic organization, and verified sources makes this a practical tool for reporters covering Eastern Shore race and diversity issues. The identified refinements point toward an even stronger final version.

=== stardem_topic_entities_notes.md ===
Star-Dem Topic Entities 10/10/2025 

For this assignment, I worked with two different models to extract entities from Star-Democrat race and diversity stories: groq/meta-llama/llama-4-maverick-17b-128e-instruct and groq/openai/gpt-oss-120b.

The prompt I created for my first run was significantly more specified than anything I had used in the prior class. This time, I really wanted it to show me geographic areas in detail while also understanding the types of people and organizations involved in these race and diversity stories.I wanted context, titles, roles, and relationships. I included specific examples in this version, showing the model exactly what I meant by "extract people with titles" by giving examples like "Mayor John Smith" and "Rev. James Williams." I also included the Maryland county reference list that Cat provided, which mapped municipalities to their counties. This was crucial because I wanted to know not just that a story mentioned "Cambridge," but that Cambridge is in Dorchester County.

The results were remarkably better than my previous assignment. When stories mentioned "Cambridge," it correctly identified Dorchester County. When "Easton" appeared, it knew that was Talbot County. However, there were still problems: the content_type field kept returning "full document" instead of useful categories, and I was getting too many "N/A" values for people, organizations, and geographic information in stories where these entities clearly existed.

For the second iteration, I made changes. I expanded content_type from just two options to fourteen specific categories: news article, feature article, enterprise story, profile, analysis, sports story, arts/culture, opinion, obituary, calendar listing, legal notice, announcement, brief, and other. I also added strong emphasis with "CRITICAL - DO NOT USE N/A UNLESS ABSOLUTELY CERTAIN" and provided detailed instructions with multiple search strategies for finding people, organizations, and authors.

The second prompt produced the best results I'd had so far, though it took the longest to process. Content types were now specific and useful, I could see what percentage of coverage was news articles, what percetange was features, and etc. The N/A problem improved, but not drastically people and organizations were still heavily n/a. 

But here's what puzzled me: the second version still had trouble finding authors, which I thought was odd because the versions I did prior to this assignment were worse in terms of content types and overall quality, but they still found the authors more reliably. It seemed like maybe I had overwhelmed the model with too many instructions, causing it to focus less on the byline while searching for all the people mentioned in the story itself.

When I examined 5-10 stories in detail, I found that basic elements like dates and titles were consistently accurate, and there were no false positives, the model wasn't inventing entities. However, significant problems emerged immediately. The most significant issue was that authors were completely missing from the author field even though their bylines appeared clearly in the content files. For example, Tony Woods wrote "Trump rebrands 'Don't Ask, Don't Tell' policy," but the author field showed "N/A." This wasn't an isolated case, it was like this for all of the stories.

The model also struggled with completeness. It missed numerous people mentioned in the stories, including Ronald Reagan in the MLK story, Julie Schablitsky (Chief Archaeologist for Maryland Department of Transportation) in the Harriet Tubman story, and multiple people in the Caroline County MLK story including student Wanda Molock and Commissioner Frank Bartz. Geographic details were sometimes missed too, like Rochester, New York in the Frederick Douglass story. Content type classification was oversimplified, narrative stories were just labeled "full article" rather than recognizing their storytelling approach.

When I compared the two models directly, I noticed some interesting differences the GPT-OSS-120b model was significantly faster quicker than the llama model. It followed structured instructions well and was excellent at using the county reference list for geographic matching. However, it was more likely to return N/A when it wasn't completely certain about something, and sometimes it was inconsistent with formatting. One story might have people listed as "Mayor Smith; Dr. Jones" and another might format it slightly differently.

The Llama model (groq/meta-llama/llama-4-maverick-17b-128e-instruct) , on the other hand, was slower but more thorough. It was better at finding people and organizations hidden in complex sentence structures. The formatting was more consistent across all stories, and it was superior at distinguishing between the different content types, it seemed to understand the difference between a news article and a feature article more intuitively. It was also less likely to give up and return N/A when information was there but not obvious. For the final analysis, I felt the improved accuracy from this model was worth the extra processing time, even though neither model solved the author extraction problem as well as I hoped.  

What the model did extract, it extracted correctly. People's names were accurate when found, and geographic data like Dorchester County and Talbot County was consistent. Talbot County was also the most frequent mentioned place with 32 mentions. The Organizations like NAACP and Delmarva Pride Center were properly identified when captured. These two were also the top two organozations mentioned in the articles (they had 3 mentions each). The patterns made sense for my topic, it mostly being in Talbot County made sense because the papaer is made there and also the organizations made sense as well becasue they were organization that fought for equality for people. 

The core problem became clear: the LLM wasn't systematically reading through the content files and transferring information to the structured fields. All the data existed in the content, authors, people, places, organizations, but the model processed each field independently rather than comprehensively scanning the narrative text. For building a functional beat book, I would need to create a model that systematically extracts all information from the content file and properly distributes it across metadata fields, with accurate filters in Datasette to make the data actually searchable and useful for journalistic research.
=== stardem_topics_notes.md ===
Star-Democrat Topic Classification 10/02/25

Approach Selection: 
I chose Option 2 because it gave me more control over the topic categories. After exploring the data with the embedding atlas and looking at sample articles, I had formed hypotheses about the main topics covered by the Star-Democrat. I wanted to see if my initial assessment aligned with what the LLM would find when systematically classifying all 200 stories.

My Topic List:
- Education
- Religion
- Animals
- Historical Information
- Sports
- Local Government
- Elections
- Courts and Public Safety
- Business and Economy
- Farming and Agriculture
- Other

Reasoning:
I selected these topics based on the distribution of stories in the sample data. While common topics like Local Government and Education were well-represented, I also noticed significant coverage of more specialized topics such as Historical Information and Animals, which reflected the publication's unique editorial focus on Eastern Shore culture and community.

Results Analysis: 
The LLM performed fairly well at assigning stories to the topics I designated. I was particularly surprised by the Historical Information section, which contained 24 stories. While this was a very niche topic, the substantial coverage reflects the Star-Democrat's focus on Eastern Shore heritage. I was also surprised by the "Other" section as this had 32 stories, as I noticed it included many community news stories. I initally realized I had forgotten to include a "Community News" tag in my original list, but I was pleased to see that the "Other" category captured most of these stories appropriately. I was also satisfied that each topic I assigned had at least 5 or more stories, which met my expectations for a balanced distribution.

The stories were generally well-categorized. For instance, the election stories were genuinely about election news, such as coverage of conservative Latino voters' reactions to Trump's return to office. The sports section also contained relevant sports content, though it focused heavily on local sports, which I think could benefit from more specific categorization in the future. Overall, I could have been clearer in distinguishing between local government news and general local community news, in order to eliminate the other section but overall I liked the responses I was given.

I chose Option 2 because I felt it was more collaborative with the LLM. I feel that if I had given the LLM complete freedom, it might have generated generic or overly broad categories. I really appreciated having the ability to set clear parameters and having the LLM match stories to those specific topics. In my experience with giving LLMs full control, I often find myself needing to edit extensively afterward, and this approach eliminated that extra work. What surprised me most was how responsive and accurate the LLM was when working within my defined framework.
